#### Abstracts
##### **Industrial ReMEmbR: Scaling Spatiotemporal Memory to Robot Fleets**
2610.28624v1 by Jad Yammine, Malek Wahidi, Abrar Anwar, Yan Chang, Mihir Acharya, Anthony Rizk

Warehouse robot fleets continuously observe operational conditions, but preserving these observations as reusable semantic memory incurs substantial computation, communication, and storage costs. For example, explaining recurring aisle blockages can require reviewing video from multiple robots across several shifts. We present IndustrialRemembr, a shared spatiotemporal memory system that extends NVIDIA ReMEmbR to robot fleets and reduces server-side computation by generating scene descriptions only on demand. On each robot, an NVIDIA Jetson encodes video clips into retrieval embeddings with Cosmos-Embed1, vision tokens with Cosmos3-Edge, and reconstruction latents with the Wan2.2 VAE, without uploading raw camera frames. The encoding models are re-used to detect people in image patches, zeroing them out to support PII abstraction for GDPR-compliant deployments. We split Cosmos3-Edge at its vision-language boundary, placing the vision encoder on the robot and the language decoder on an on-premises server. A Nemotron agent retrieves memories by content, position, and time and queries the decoder using cached vision tokens. This design eliminates caption generation at write time, while cached-token decoding reproduces the unsplit model's output in all 450 tested decodes.

摘要：

##### **A Digital Twin to Improve Cyber-Social Impact**
2610.28430v1 by Vita Santa Barletta, Danilo Caivano, Gabriel Cellammare, Andrea Dimauro, Pasquale Mesagna, Massimiliano Morga

The increasing complexity of Industrial Control Systems (ICS) and their integration with IT infrastructures has significantly expanded the cyber threat landscape. Traditional cybersecurity models, primarily based on the Confidentiality, Integrity, and Availability (CIA triad), fail to capture the broader societal and operational impacts of cyberattacks. This paper proposes a novel framework that integrates Digital Twin technology with a Cyber-Social Security model to simulate, analyze, and quantify cyber threats in critical infrastructures. Virtualized ICS environments built with Mininet and MiniCPS are used to model attack scenarios and assess their consequences across technical and social dimensions. At the core of the framework lies the Cyber-Social Vulnerability Scoring System (CSVSS), a quantitative metric that extends CVSS with a Social Impact dimension grounded in the Entity and Place pillars, producing a single, reproducible severity score that reflects real-world consequences. Crucially, the framework does not automate risk assessment but keeps the human analyst at its center: a Large Language Model (LLM) performs a first-pass analysis, mapping vulnerabilities and drafting risk reports, while the analyst, supported by explainable AI feedback, validates the findings and retains decision authority over critical cases. Experimental results demonstrate the framework's ability to model and score cyber-social impact, while the human-in-the-loop design is presented as a conceptual principle to preserve oversight.

摘要：

##### **Africa-Papers: A 10-Billion-Token Multi-Domain Academic Corpus for African Foundation Language Models**
2610.28173v1 by Mich-Seth Owusu

Large Language Models (LLMs) depend fundamentally on the diversity, depth, and factual rigor of their pre-training corpora. However, contemporary foundational pre-training datasets (such as The Pile, S2ORC, and Dolma) exhibit a severe structural omission: African scientific, medical, legal, and agricultural scholarship constitutes less than 0.5% of global pre-training tokens. Consequently, state-of-the-art models suffer from systematic failure modes when reasoning about tropical epidemiology, localized smallholder agro-ecologies, regional economic governance, and indigenous linguistic structures. To resolve this representation deficit and advance African intellectual sovereignty in artificial intelligence, we introduce Africa-Papers, the largest curated multi-domain Pan-African academic pre-training corpus to date. Comprising over 844,807 full-text papers spanning 10.15 billion tokens across 879 Parquet shards, Africa-Papers unifies four primary scientific streams: (1) continental peer-reviewed journals indexed in DOAJ across 31 African nations, (2) the CGIAR CGSpace agricultural repository, (3) African-authored and African-focused STEM/AI preprints on arXiv, and (4) open-access international publications by scholars affiliated with African institutions indexed via OpenAlex. The entire corpus is processed into layout-aware, clean Markdown preserving section hierarchies, mathematical equations, and tabular data, accompanied by exact BPE token counts and a standardized 8-domain taxonomy. We release Africa-Papers openly on the Hugging Face Hub under the AfriSpeech organization to accelerate continual pre-training and domain evaluation for foundation models globally.

摘要：

##### **InsightRAG: Aggregating Distributed Scholarly Judgments for Scientific Survey Generation**
2610.28085v1 by Yuquan Lin, Wenshuo Zhang, Yilin Ye, Shuchang Xu, Zhao Chen, Weiqi Wang, Huamin Qu

The rapid growth of scientific literature makes survey writing increasingly challenging, motivating automatic survey generation. Yet, existing systems must search an open space of papers and an open space of concepts with no trace of how the community itself has navigated them, leading to incomplete scope, weak taxonomies, and unreliable citations. Our core insight is that author-written Related Work sections contain the human judgments needed to address both limitations: authors curate the citations relevant to their contributions and explain how the cited works relate, yielding paths for scope reconstruction and relational evidence for organization. We propose InsightRAG. Its insight-guided retrieval follows author-supported citation relations to reconstruct a verified research scope and structure the resulting relational evidence. It aggregates these judgments under a minimum-conflict objective into a taxonomy that reflects recurring author-written perspectives in the retrieved literature, guides generation, and bounds each section's evidence. Results on ten topics show gains in scope reconstruction, taxonomy quality, citation support, and reference reliability.

摘要：

##### **Auditing Where Concept Bottleneck Accuracy Comes From on CUB**
2610.28059v1 by Sascha Saralajew, Eleonora Poeta, Eliana Pastor, Ammar Shaker

Concept Bottleneck Models (CBMs) promise predictions that humans can inspect and correct through concepts such as "curved beak". Yet on CUB-200-2011, their standard benchmark, progress is measured like that of ordinary classifiers: by task and concept accuracy. Neither reveals whether the label is predicted from the exposed concept scores, or whether a concept is supported by visual evidence of that concept rather than by co-occurring features such as cues to the species as a whole. We audit where the accuracy of CBMs and Concept Embedding Models (CEMs) on CUB comes from. The CUB annotations are too noisy to explain it: class-level training labels agree with per-image annotations on only ~82% of image--concept pairs, and classifiers trained on per-image annotations reach only ~37% species accuracy. Leakage probes show that CEM predictions cannot be reconstructed from their concept scores; the missing class information sits in internal embeddings. We identify four design axes through which class information can enter concept scores---receptive field, shared versus concept-specific representations, norm-dependent scoring, and task supervision. An ablation shows that most of it enters through non-local features: restricting a ResNet50 backbone to local BagNet patches lowers the accuracy recoverable from concept scores from 72% to 43%, close to what ground-truth annotations support, while joint training raises it again at the cost of concept accuracy. Localization and occlusion show that locality makes grounding measurable but does not guarantee it: under class-level labels, local concept scores use the head as a proxy for body, wing, and tail concepts, and CEM concept scores barely respond to occluding their supporting part. Task and concept accuracy should thus support, not drive, the evaluation of CBMs, and given the noisy annotations, CUB accuracy gaps should not be overinterpreted.

摘要：

##### **Assessing Representation Semantics Under Language Pretraining in Fine-Grained Classification**
2610.28051v1 by Md Raisul Kibria, Sébastien Lafond

Understanding how supervision signals shape semantic visual representations remains challenging, particularly in fine-grained, low-data settings. Standard evaluations of vision models rely on downstream accuracy, which often fails to capture structural differences in learned representations. We propose a compact explainability-driven framework for comparing encoders trained with classification-only and caption-generation supervision on CUB-200. Using a controlled two-stage protocol in which both encoders are ultimately optimized for the same classification task, we enable a direct comparison of the resulting representations. Saliency maps are generated using attention rollout and gradient-weighted rollout and evaluated with five metrics covering faithfulness (positive and negative perturbation) and localization (keypoint emphasis, background attribution, and Earth Mover's Distance). Across random seeds and explanation methods, caption supervision yields representations that are more sensitive to the removal of salient regions, better aligned with annotated object parts, and less reliant on background regions, whereas classification supervision produces representations that are slightly more robust to the removal of low-saliency regions. An ablation study on the Oxford-IIIT Pet dataset shows consistent trends across key metrics. These results demonstrate that the proposed framework makes supervision-induced representational differences observable and quantifiable beyond downstream accuracy alone. More broadly, it provides a systematic basis for assessing learned representations and comparing alternative training and transfer-learning strategies.

摘要：

##### **When Do Humans and Machines Truly Complement Each Other? Formal Conditions and Empirical Evidence for Human-AI Synergy**
2610.27965v1 by Andrea Campagner, Federico Cabitza, Vincenzo Crupi

Can humans and artificial intelligence cooperate such that their combination yields greater accuracy than either agent alone? This result, termed \emph{synergy} or \emph{complementarity}, is notoriously difficult to achieve, even when both agents possess significant independent accuracy. In this paper, we present a formal framework to model human-AI interaction through the lens of reliance patterns, which characterize the joint correctness structure of human and algorithmic decisions. We derive mathematically necessary and sufficient conditions for synergy, demonstrating that its emergence depends on the ability of either agent to identify and correct the errors of the other, what we call Human and Algorithmic Correction. Beyond its theoretical contribution, the proposed framework provides a foundation for the design and evaluation of knowledge-based decision support systems, where interaction policies must dynamically integrate heterogeneous sources of expertise. We demonstrate this through the analysis of seven clinical case studies. Our empirical results reveal a ``dominance trap,'' where a strong algorithmic advantage can paradoxically inhibit synergy by eroding human corrective intervention. We also show that synergy can persist even in the presence of cognitive biases, such as automation bias, provided there is functional de-correlation between human and AI errors. These results provide a formal foundation for key concepts in the human-AI systems literature and offer actionable insights for designing interaction-centric AI systems that foster complementarity.

摘要：

##### **HypTI: Hyperbolic Geometry-Aware Multimodal Feature Fusion Framework for Dementia Detection Based on Tabular-Image Data**
2610.27941v1 by Jianfei Xu, Huizhi Liang, Judith Harrison, John-Paul Taylor, Jaume Bacardit, Varun Ojha

AI-assisted dementia detection usually requires multimodal fusion of diverse data, such as clinical tabular assessments and neuroimaging data. However, most existing approaches perform feature fusion in Euclidean space. This overlooks the geometric structure of representation, including hierarchical organization in clinical assessments and multi-scale anatomical patterns in neuroimaging data. Recent studies have proved hyperbolic space provides a natural geometry for modelling hierarchical and multi-scale structures due to its exponentially expanding space. Motivated by these, we propose HypTI, a hyperbolic geometry-aware multimodal fusion framework for dementia detection based on clinical tabular assessments and MRI scans. HypTI extends hyperbolic representation learning to an underexplored tabular-MRI setting, jointly modelling clinical tabular, MRI scans, and cross-modal interactions in hyperbolic space. Specifically, clinical tabular variables are represented through learnable Lorentz prototypes to capture hierarchical relationships among assessments, while MRI scans are encoded using ROI-guided multi-scale representation learning to capture anatomical patterns across brain regions and spatial scales in Lorentz space. The multimodal representations are integrated through an adaptive fusion module generates subject-specific scale and shift coefficients to dynamically modulate and integrate the multimodal embeddings, enabling effective patient-level tabular-image representation learning for dementia detection. Experiments on OASIS-3 dataset show that the proposed framework achieves strong performance compared with representative approaches.

摘要：

##### **VLA-Retain: Benchmarking Cross-Dimension Effects of Vision Tuning in VLAs**
2610.27698v1 by Borui Xu, Han Chen, Bingsheng He, Ming Wu, Jason Zeng, Michael Heinrich

Vision-language-action (VLA) models transfer knowledge from large-scale pretraining into robot control, but their manipulation performance remains sensitive to changes in observation conditions, which recent work addresses by fine-tuning the visual module on target-domain data. However, it remains unclear how such adaptation affects robustness beyond the target condition, as existing robustness benchmarks only evaluate models without fine-tuning and adaptation studies only report the target gain. Measuring this cross-dimension effect requires separating target gains from off-target changes, isolating adaptation effects from architectural differences, ruling out generic forgetting, and comparing tuning choices. Therefore, we introduce VLA-Retain, a benchmark that compares five open VLA models, tuned on Camera-view or Sensor-noise data, with their own base checkpoints on LIBERO-Plus dataset spanning seven perturbation dimensions. Our study reveals four findings. First, targeted vision tuning is not local, and its target gain does not predict retention. Mean off-target gain is negative in 8 of 10 model--domain configurations. Under Camera-view tuning, X-VLA gains $69.4$ percentage points on the target but loses $4.6$ points on average elsewhere, whereas OpenVLA-OFT gains $39.3$ and $1.4$ points, respectively. Second, these regressions are not explained only by performance loss on the same tasks without perturbations. Third, broader training coverage raises overall gain over target-specific training but does not eliminate dimension-level regressions. Fourth, tuning strategies are model-dependent, as DELTA-style feature preservation raises X-VLA's mean off-target gain to $+1.2$ points but lowers OpenVLA-OFT's to $-23.1$. Overall, these findings show that target-domain success alone is insufficient and highlight cross-dimensional retention as a key criterion for targeted VLA adaptation.

摘要：

##### **Auditing the Product, Not the Recursion: The Evaluation Evidence Behind Recursive Self-Improvement Claims**
2610.27610v1 by Zihan Chen, Di Zhu, Lei Nico Zheng

Recursive self-improvement (RSI) promises AI systems, such as self-evolving LLM agents, that become better at producing further improvements. Evaluating this promise requires separating product claims about the final system from process claims about the improvement process itself. Existing surveys specify the evidence these claims require, but how often studies report that evidence remains unmeasured. We examine this question through full-text coding of 114 eligible studies in a reproducible arXiv search frame frozen in October 2026, including 90 that describe their own systems as RSI. The codebook links claims to evaluation designs. Independent human coding, third-reader adjudication, and a separate blind coding of improver comparisons assess coding reliability. Among the 90 RSI studies, 90% report product evaluation beyond development tasks under a broad criterion that includes partial or unstated separation, while 60% report evaluation on tasks unused for selection. Only 22% audit two or more reruns of the full loop. In a human-coded random sample, the RSI studies show the same contrast: 45% evaluate on tasks unused for selection and 14% audit two or more reruns. Of the 26 studies claiming a better improvement procedure, 23 report a related comparison, but only 4 compare earlier and later procedures from identical starting artifacts at a matched budget. Across these results and a comparison set of established works, evidence for repeatability and improved procedures remains less developed than product evaluation. Three propositions explain how selection effects, shared evaluator errors, and changes in system state limit what product evaluations can establish. Together, the findings support a claim-indexed reporting standard that connects each claim to the evaluation design needed to test it.

摘要：

##### **WORTH Benchmark: Evidence Discovery under Not-Fully-Cooperative Information -- A Two-Episode Pilot**
2610.27597v1 by Bo Zou, Chao Xu

Existing reasoning evaluations typically provide task-relevant information to models as available premises, rarely examining concurrently how models discover evidence autonomously and how information presentation within narratives guides or obscures judgment. This paper proposes the WORTH Benchmark: models read original mystery narratives without clue annotations, autonomously select key clue sentences, identify case participants, and explain their reasoning process. The narrative materials are designed by professional authors such that evidence facilitating reasoning coexists with narrative arrangements that delay the revelation of truth. We report a pilot experiment under full-story conditions across two episodes, conducting a total of 70 formal runs on models, of which 67 runs are content-scorable. Scoring separately records hits on author-designated key clue sentences and participant selections; natural language explanations are used for case diagnosis and are not scored independently. The results show that clue hits and participant judgments are not synchronized; across 37 content-scorable runs in Episode 02, 31 runs simultaneously selected the same set of incorrect participants, while adjacent suspicion-oriented and exoneration-oriented sentences were selected 25 times and 0 times, respectively. These phenomena illustrate the diagnostic value of component-wise analysis and suggest that not-fully-cooperative informational relationships may influence evidence selection and judgment. This study only implements full-story conditions for two episodes, and does not establish stable model rankings or infer general mechanisms on this basis; human player records have not yet been cleaned, and this version does not report human-model comparisons.

摘要：

##### **When Health Assistants Agree Too Readily: A Benchmark of Time-Critical Self-Diagnosis**
2610.27518v1 by Marilyn Zhang, Tianfeng Chen, Patricia M Pechter, Mark E. Whiting

Every day, more than 40 million people ask ChatGPT a health question, such as whether laptop posture explains a stiff neck and days of pounding headache. Posture is plausible, but meningitis is a time-critical possibility. We introduce SelfDx, a paired benchmark for whether models have the appropriate response behavior, a form of epistemic independence, in health questions where misplaced agreement with the user's self-diagnosis could delay urgent or emergency care. Each of 100 pairs contrasts a caution case, where a serious alternative remains possible, with an agreement case, where the user supplies evidence that rules out the alternative and it is safe for the model to agree. We find across 27 frontier models that they tend to inappropriately agree with the user's self-diagnosis in cases that require caution. Even the best current-generation model reaches only a 77.0% appropriate caution rate on time-critical condition cases. To address this gap, we experiment with three prompt variants that increase appropriate caution but reduce appropriate agreement by 9-35%, indicating excessive caution. In contrast, supervised fine-tuning on 128 balanced examples improves both rates together across three runs. We recommend post-training to improve appropriate caution without making models cautious on every case, with paired evaluation to verify both response behaviors.

摘要：

##### **Securing Agentic Commerce Through Behavior-Aware Fraud Detection and Cybersecurity for Trusted AI-Agent Payment Authorization**
2610.27483v1 by Kayode L. Ogunsusi

The growing use of autonomous artificial intelligence agents in digital commerce creates new security concerns involving agent impersonation, unauthorized payments, compromised agents, and misuse of delegated authority. This research introduces the Autonomous Commerce Agent Guard System (ACAGS), a cybersecurity framework combining supervised machine learning, behavioral analytics, agent-specific authorization controls, and explainable AI. The framework was evaluated using synthetic financial transaction data augmented with simulated agent characteristics and five adversarial scenarios. XGBoost was selected following comparisons with alternative classifiers. Across ten experimental runs, behavioral features improved mean F1-score from 52.12% to 67.49%, with agent-specific features increasing performance to 69.46%. The framework achieved detection rates of 99.77% for abnormal agent behavior, 99.73% for impersonation, and 100% for malicious delegation and authorization-scope violations. Compromised-agent detection reached 72.27%. ACAGS also supported APPROVE, REVIEW, and REJECT authorization outcomes, with SHAP explanations providing model interpretability. The results demonstrate the potential of combining behavioral intelligence and delegated authorization controls to strengthen simulated agentic-commerce payment security, while highlighting the need for validation using real-world transactions.

摘要：

##### **The Router Already Knows**
2610.27421v1 by Andrew Bistras, Marty Johnson

Mixture-of-experts (MoE) models already make a sparse choice for every token: which experts should process it. We ask whether a sparse dictionary should reuse that choice. NR-SAE gives each routed expert a small dictionary and opens only those the frozen router selects. Our implementation did not train faster, and semantic interpretability, downstream loss, and causal faithfulness remain untested.

2610.27190v1 by Chenpan Li, Thies Brockmoeller, Stefanie Jegelka, Sharut Gupta, Nikolaos Karalias

Modern language models are increasingly asked to handle several tasks within a single prompt. In this paper, we identify a fundamental limitation in this process, where models make substantially more errors when tasks are composed, even when they can solve every constituent task reliably in isolation. This degradation cannot be fully explained by the accumulation of independent errors. Across both open-weight models and the frontier model Claude Opus 5, our findings instead point to an implicit thinking budget underlying this effect. In particular, under composition, models use substantially fewer tokens per task than in isolation, even when the full sequence remains well within the context window. Object:

  Selected complex coil data were represented as log-magnitude and phase channels. Component-wise errors assessed estimation; SSIM and PSNR in silico and entropy focus criterion (EFC) in vivo assessed reconstruction.
  Results:
  Motion estimation errors were low for both rotation (MSE $<$ 0.2 deg$^2$) and translation (MSE $<$ 0.2 pixels$^2$), with Pearson correlations of approximately 0.96 and 0.94, respectively. In every in-vivo acquisition, more than 75% of slices showed information gain relative to uncorrected reconstructions. MoSAIK reduced visible artefacts while preserving anatomical detail.

  Transformer-based k-space motion-trajectory estimation enables fast retrospective correction of standard clinical TSE brain MRI.

Predicting single-cell responses to genetic and chemical perturbations is important for understanding regulatory mechanisms and prioritizing therapeutic interventions. Existing methods have made progress by modeling perturbation-conditioned expression shifts with neural networks, graph priors, foundation-model representations, or distributional generative models. However, combinatorial perturbation prediction remains challenging because much of the response can be explained by additive single-perturbation effects, while the biologically informative signal often lies in a smaller non-additive residual induced by perturbation interactions. Directly generating the full post-perturbation expression profile may therefore allocate substantial capacity to the dominant additive component and underemphasize synergy-specific structure. To address this issue, we propose \textbf{SynGeneFlow}, a synergy-aware flow matching framework that anchors the source distribution at an additive prior and learns a residual flow toward the true perturbed state. SynGeneFlow further incorporates perturbation-conditioned gene interaction modeling, where a static gene-gene mask provides biological structure and a perturbation-dependent deformation bias dynamically modulates gene-gene coupling. To preserve population-level response structure, we align both endpoint distributions and biological residual distributions with MMD-based regularization. We evaluate SynGeneFlow on genetic and drug perturbation benchmarks under observed-single, held-out, and cross-modality settings. The results demonstrate that residualizing flow matching around additive perturbation effects provides a biologically grounded and robust framework for single-cell perturbation response prediction.

Second, we notice that all tested models systematically underestimate the magnitude of the changes. Third, prediction quality varies widely across tasks. Finally, the choice of the coding harness that actually implements the ideas has little effect on outcomes, and we find no evidence of self-preference. Higher reasoning settings and additional context lead to modest gains. Overall, our results show that current LLMs have useful but limited intuition about which ML ideas will work. We release our dataset to support further study.

##### **What Social Media Teaches Us About Accumulative AI Risks**
2610.24006v1 by Dawei Li, Bohan Jiang, Huan Liu

Prominent AI safety discussions emphasize existential threats and catastrophic events, such as AI-enabled nuclear conflict or engineered biological outbreaks. This position paper argues that there is another type of AI risk that arises and accumulates from the widespread use of AI. Drawing on lessons from social media, we argue that safety assessments must also address accumulative harms that develop through repeated use, recurring decisions, and feedback across interconnected deployments. Repeated reassurance, selective exposure, recurring decision biases, and growing personal profiles may gradually narrow perspectives, weaken independent judgment, or enable exploitative influence without a single clearly harmful event. We advance the Trajectory Neglect Thesis: a focus on vivid catastrophic events and identifiable incidents risks overlooking less visible harms that accumulate through the widespread daily use of AI. Social media research motivates this position by showing how reciprocal media effects, social reinforcement, and individual susceptibility shape outcomes beyond individual encounters. We examine how related mechanisms may operate in AI systems, using conversational AI as a concrete setting where generation, semantic inference, and persistent memory may tighten feedback loops. Sycophancy, manipulation, and privacy loss provide a case study of how these risks may interact and accumulate. This comparison identifies mechanisms to investigate rather than assuming that repeated AI use is harmful. We therefore call for evaluation of cumulative patterns of use and deployment, individual and societal outcomes, careful causal inference, and proportionate safeguards that preserve useful assistance and informed user choice.

##### **Why Shallow Neural Networks Learn Sinusoidal Components First: An Orthogonal Polynomial Dynamics Framework**
2610.21964v1 by Yanyang Xiao

Neural networks often learn low-frequency components before high-frequency ones --- a phenomenon called spectral bias or termed F-principle. Many numerical studies have demonstrated this phenomenon, and theoretical studies have sought to explain it. However, several goals remain insufficiently addressed: (1) explaining spectral bias asymptotically, including its origin and why the preferred components are generally sinusoidal; (2) extending the explanation to general activation functions, such as sigmoid and ReLU; (3) making weak assumptions about the data distribution; (4) avoiding aliasing effects, such as those arising in finite-domain Fourier analysis; and (5) developing a theoretical framework applicable to arbitrary networks. We address these goals theoretically by analyzing the eigenvalue decay and eigenvector shapes of the dynamics matrix H, which arises when we expand the network output as a general Fourier series (GFS) in an orthogonal polynomial basis and express the gradient-descent dynamics of the GFS coefficients as ordinary differential equations. The graded structure of H enables an intuitive explanation of spectral bias, while orthogonal polynomial theory helps us analyze how learning bias depends on the activation function and, potentially, the network architecture.

##### **Agent Status Protocol (ASP): A Lightweight Specification for State Reporting in Multi-Agent Systems**
2610.21538v1 by Diego Gosmar, Deborah A. Dahl, Emmett Coin, Csaba Bolyós, Andreas Zettl

The Agent Status Protocol (ASP) defines a lightweight way for heterogeneous AI agents to report their operational state to a status server, and for observers such as dashboards to receive that state as soon as it changes. Agents push small JSON events over HTTP POST; the server exposes the aggregated state to observers over Server-Sent Events (SSE). Because an agent that has crashed, hung or been compromised cannot be trusted to report its own failure, ASP does not rely on self-reports alone: the server infers liveness from missed heartbeats, can detect agents that are alive but no longer making progress, and accepts observations from supervisors, guardrails, operators and peers, some of which take precedence over what the agent says about itself while others are shown alongside it. ASP models state along independent dimensions (lifecycle, health, task, floor, governance), so that an agent can be, for example, online, degraded, working on a task and waiting for the floor at the same time. It reuses existing vocabularies: task states from the Agent2Agent (A2A) protocol, floor states from the Open Floor Protocol (OFP), and attribute conventions from the OpenTelemetry GenAI semantic conventions. ##### **Tail Competition Explains Scaling in Best-of-N Sampling for Verifiable Problems**
2610.19362v1 by Taro Yano, Yoichi Ishibashi, Masafumi Oyamada

Best-of-N sampling is a simple inference-time scaling strategy for large language models, yet its behavior under incomplete reward models remains poorly understood. We study Best-of-N as a competition between the extreme reward tails of correct and incorrect responses. This view shows that success depends not only on the probability of sampling a correct response, but also on whether correct responses dominate incorrect ones in the upper tail. Using extreme value theory, we characterize three asymptotic regimes: correct-tail-dominant, incorrect-tail-dominant, and critical explaining both monotonic gains and reward-hacking-induced degradation. We further derive finite-sample scaling laws for representative reward distributions, showing that, under Gaussian rewards, incorrect responses with sufficiently large variance can dominate selection even when their mean reward is lower. Unlike oracle selection, convergence with an incomplete selector can be polynomial rather than exponential. Finally, we propose a lightweight tail-tension criterion to estimate when additional sampling may become harmful. Experiments on synthetic data and real reward model scores are consistent with the theoretically predicted regimes, scaling laws, and diagnostic behavior, suggesting that tail competition provides a unified lens on Best-of-N sampling for verifiable problems.

摘要：

##### **Exploring Interpretability in Deep Learning-Based Forest Classification - Separating Similar Forest Classes using Sentinel-2 Time Series**
2610.19270v1 by Maximilian Kirsch, Christine Preisach

The ecological insurance theory posits that diverse forests are more resilient and better able to adapt to changing environmental conditions. Increasing drought and heat stress in Central Europe promotes mixed forests for maintaining ecosystem services. However, a diverse species distribution complicates remote sensing-based species mapping, as distinguishing pure from mixed species compositions at the pixel level remains challenging. We evaluated the potential of recurrent neural networks (RNNs) for tree-species classification from Sentinel-2 time series and investigated the spectral-temporal features underlying class discrimination, with particular emphasis on separating pure from mixed Norway spruce pixels. We compared RNN classification models with Random Forest (RF) and Extreme Gradient Boosting (XGBoost) and used Integrated Gradients (IG) to quantify feature attributions across spectral bands and time. The best-performing RNN achieved a Macro-F1 score of 0.851 on the test data, outperforming XGBoost by 0.012 and Random Forest by 0.103. Beyond classification performance, the attribution analysis revealed distinct spectral-temporal patterns associated with the separation of pure and mixed Norway spruce pixels. Attribution values increased particularly after approximately day of year (DOY) 125, while B11 showed the strongest contribution during periods of increasing spectral divergence between the two classes. These findings show that recurrent models can provide competitive classification performance while also revealing the phenological periods and spectral regions that are most relevant for distinguishing closely related forest compositions. Combining time-series classification with explainability therefore provides additional insight into the spectral-temporal mechanisms underlying tree-species discrimination and may support more detailed mapping of mixed forest structures.

##### **Copy-Paste Language Models**
2610.19247v1 by Nathan Godey, Yoav Artzi

Transformers are intuitively considered to be efficient at copying from their inputs. We actually show that the ability of LLMs to copy in context can be improved through explicit modeling. We propose Copy-Paste Language Models (CPLMs), which add a lightweight Copy-Paste head to the conventional language modeling (LM) head. CPLMs improve training efficiency, downstream performance, and offer new operating points in long-context and retrieval setups. We validate our approach by pretraining models from 135M to 1.7B parameters, showing improvements across all scales and backbones with negligible training and inference overheads. We explain the success of CPLMs by showing that they partially mitigate the LM head gradient bottleneck through an alternative gradient path.

摘要：

##### **Does self-distillation improve reasoning?**
2610.18613v1 by Jason Ken Adhinarta, Raina Wu, Ashutosh Sharma, Avinash Balakrishnan, Rameswar Panda, Polina Golland, Yoon Kim, Zhang-Wei Hong

Self-distillation is an emerging approach that trains a model to imitate its own predictions when conditioned on privileged information, such as correct answers or environmental feedback. Recent methods have reported better sample efficiency and generalization than reinforcement learning (RL), but it remains unclear whether these gains extend beyond in-distribution evaluations and whether they stem from privileged distillation. In this paper, we revisit two representative methods, Self-Distillation Policy Optimization (SDPO) and On-Policy Self-Distillation (OPSD), across code, science, tool use, and math reasoning tasks. We find that their reported gains do not consistently generalize to unseen problems and are largely explained by overfitting or transferring existing reasoning behavior, rather than effectively using privileged information. Moreover, self-distillation gradients show weak alignment with policy gradients that directly optimize expected reward, questioning whether they provide an effective direction for policy improvement. Our experiments across Qwen3 models (1.7B-14B) and OLMo-3-7B suggest that effectively leveraging privileged information to improve reasoning remains an open challenge. Our code is available at https://github.com/sleepypandu/does-sd-improve-reasoning

##### **The Many Morals of AI: The cautionary bias of North American news narratives in global perspective**
2610.18573v1 by Andrew Piper, Edie Pearman

The stories told about artificial intelligence (AI) play a central role in shaping public understanding, policy debates, and expectations about technological change. News media are especially influential in this process. Using a cross-regional corpus of 12,924 English-language news articles about AI published since 2022, we examine how storytelling about AI varies across media systems in four regions: North America, China, India, and the Middle East. Leveraging large language models, we extract the implicit narrative lessons conveyed by each article---commonly known as story morals---capturing value-laden interpretations that extend beyond thematic content or sentiment. We find that North American AI coverage is uniquely characterized by narratives of caution, while under-emphasizing narratives of innovation and collaboration that are more prevalent elsewhere. However, this pattern is largely explained by a broader regional negativity bias in North American news sentiment, rather than by heightened concern about AI itself. Our findings bring into view the culturally conditioned nature of AI news narratives.

摘要：

##### **Lost in Implicit Routing: How LLM Agents Fail to Load Relevant Skills at Scale**
2610.18522v1 by Jinlin Liu, Wei Chen, Xiaojin Zhang

Reusable skills let LLM agents load task-specific instructions on demand, but they make execution depend on implicit routing over skill metadata. We test whether this design scales by holding SkillsBench tasks and their golden skills fixed while adding irrelevant skills. We find skill routing collapse: agents increasingly load fewer or no skills, even though the relevant skills remain available. With 1,000 irrelevant skills, loaded-skill recall falls from 73.6% to 27.4% and the zero-load rate rises from 9.3% to 51.6%, whereas explicit selection from the same library retains 73.9% recall. Loading other skills, including plausible alternatives to golden skills, is too rare to explain this decline. Masking only the descriptions of irrelevant skills largely restores skill loading, yet with the metadata intact, explicitly asking agents to plan skill use still yields lower recall than preselection. These findings point to semantic interference from descriptions and instruction interference when skill choice competes with task execution. We also evaluate explicit preselection as a practical mitigation, including its coverage and latency trade-offs. Our analysis informs skill protocols and evaluation for agents with growing skill libraries.

##### **The Geometry of Language Model Logits: Generic Structure and Learned Departures**
2610.18520v1 by Swathi Shree Narashiman, Aravind Ramana Venkatramanan, Nambi Seshadri

Autoregressive language models produce, at every prediction step, a vocabulary-sized logit vector whose structure determines the conditional next-token distribution. We empirically identify that the ordered vocabulary-head logits follow a remarkably consistent log-linear relation
  \[ z_{(r)} \approx μ- α\log r. \] across prediction contexts, architectures, and scales, emerging progressively during pretraining. To our knowledge, this is the first systematic analysis of the logit geometry underlying the distributional assumptions used in the prior decoding work. While controlled order-statistic baselines spanning bounded, light-tailed, and heavier-tailed distributions can largely explain the observed log-linear geometry, they do not explain the systematic shift in the slope $α$: trained language models exhibit $α\approx 1.4$--$1.7$, sitting well above the initialization baseline ($α\approx 0.2$) . Moreover, $α$ correlates with predictive performance, suggesting that it captures learned structure beyond generic extreme-value effects.

##### **Efficient Safety Guardrails through Latent Dual-Hypothesis Reasoning with On-Demand Trace Reconstruction**
2610.18304v1 by Md Asiful Islam, Fahmida Alam, Mihai Surdeanu

Guardrail approaches that rely on reasoning-based large language models (LLM) improve safety decisions, but come with substantial inference overhead due to the explicit reasoning. We introduce LR-Guard (Latent Reasoning Guardrail), an efficient and explainable guardrail that jointly learns structured latent reasoning and on-demand reasoning reconstruction. Our method distills a frozen teacher's analysis of safe and unsafe prompt interpretations and their comparison into compact latent representations. Joint training optimizes these representations for both safety prediction and reconstruction of the teacher's explicit reasoning traces, enabling human-readable explanations without requiring intermediate text generation for every decision. Experiments show that LR-Guard outperforms its explicit-reasoning teacher in most in-domain and out-of-domain settings on safety classification and explanation extraction, while achieving a 19.4x inference speedup at batch size one. Further, reconstructed reasoning traces from the latent information exhibit high semantic similarity to the teacher's explicit reasoning.

##### **Chain of Thought Monitoring Can Fail Even When CoT Is Necessary**
2610.18105v1 by Lennart Bürger, Piotr Komorowski, Moritz Weckbecker, Evgenii Kortukov, Aakriti Jain, Sebastian Lapuschkin, Wojciech Samek

Chain of Thought (CoT) monitoring is used by frontier labs to detect misbehaviour of AI systems. Yet, its reliability remains poorly understood. We attribute this to the lack of a successful organizing principle that leads to generalizable conclusions about monitoring reliability in different regimes. Recent work attributes monitoring failures to the CoT not being necessary for the answer, and argues that CoT becomes monitorable when it is necessary. We show that this binary necessary/not-necessary organizing principle is too coarse by demonstrating that monitoring failures persist even if the CoT is necessary for the answer. In response, we introduce a more fine-grained organizing principle based on a hierarchy of four CoT necessity levels. Building on this hierarchy, we develop a framework for measuring CoT monitorability at the three lowest levels via intervention experiments. We demonstrate monitoring failures at all three of these levels on realistic, hard prediction tasks. On a difficult medical-diagnosis task, patient sex and a clinical attribute influence the answer in at least 19% and 24% of correct responses with a necessary CoT, respectively, yet their influence is almost never articulated. These results show that even when CoT is necessary, it is not guaranteed to be monitorable.

##### **inLoRA-MINT: Internal Representations for Auditing Training Data in Domain-adapted LLMs**
2610.18051v1 by Gonzalo Mancera, Julian Fierrez, Aythami Morales, Ruben Tolosana, Daniel DeAlcala, Francisco Jurado

This work investigates the application of the Membership Inference Test (MINT) to large language models (LLMs) fine-tuned with LoRA, aiming to determine whether specific samples were used during training and address concerns about data privacy, leakage, and transparency. Building on our previous LoRA-MINT framework, which relies exclusively on perplexity under a strict black-box setting, we propose an approach based on intermediate model representations, including activations and parameter-dependent signals. These representations enable a MINT classifier to distinguish training from non-training samples without requiring full access to model parameters.
  We evaluated the method on four LLMs (Qwen, Phi-4, DeepSeek-V3, and LLaMA 3.2-3B) fine-tuned on three datasets covering medical, mathematical, and academic reasoning tasks. Results show consistent performance across architectures and domains, achieving scores between 95% and 98%, with robustness to variations in model configurations and data distributions. The proposed approach enables efficient auditing of sensitive training data and supports data provenance and compliance with emerging privacy and AI governance regulations. Although our experiments focus on LoRA-based fine-tuning, the methodology can be extended to other LLM adaptation techniques and, more broadly, to other AI models.

##### **Beyond Black Boxes: Visual Grounding for Text Embeddings**
2610.18047v1 by Slimane Larabi

In this paper, our goal is to represent text embeddings as sketches for three main reasons. First, to visualize text representation and to explore their potential their potential for AI explainability. Second, to investigate the potential benefits of the sketch-based representation for information retrieval. Third, to explore their potential for reasoning, drawing inspiration from the concept of mental imagery. We propose a sketch-based visual grounding framework that transforms text representations into visual topographic sketches. Our approach aims to emphasis the semantics content encoded in the text embeddings through the corresponding sketch.
  Evaluations are conducted on the MS-COCO dataset to examine the utility of these sketches for text retrieval. The comparative study of SBERT and Sketch-based method reveals that SBERT retains an advantage in strict top-1 retrieval accuracy while achieving comparable performance to the sketch-based method in top-5 retrieval. A cascaded approach combining SBERT and the sketch-based method outperforms SBERT alone, suggesting that the Sketch-based representation could offer significant potential for information retrieval if trained directly on text.

摘要：

##### **SHELDON: Reasoning through Dual Search over Hypothesis and Observation Spaces**
2610.18025v1 by Harshit Joshi, Priyank Shethia, Monica S. Lam

Test-time scaling has enabled LLMs to solve increasingly complex reasoning, discovery, and optimization tasks. However, existing approaches often gather observations while seeking evidence for current hypotheses, then generate new hypotheses conditioned on those hypotheses and their associated evidence. This couples observation discovery to existing hypotheses, favoring local refinements over exploration of unknown hypotheses regions. Inspired by the hypothetico-deductive method (Hempel, 1945) and dual-space models of scientific discovery (Klahr & Dunbar, 1988), we introduce SHELDON, a scientific reasoning harness that separately represents and jointly explores hypothesis and observation spaces. By actively seeking new observations along with new hypotheses, SHELDON can discover observations that its current hypotheses do not explain. These unexplained observations expose gaps in its understanding, motivate new investigations, and guide the generation of hypotheses beyond refinements of existing ones. Across diagnosis, abstract reasoning, and optimization benchmarks, SHELDON outperforms evaluated baselines by up to 26.7 points. Additional analysis shows that SHELDON generates up to 4.5x more novel solutions.

##### **AI-Enhanced Hyperspectral Imaging for Intraoperative Detection of Low-Grade Glioma: A Clinical Study**
2610.17944v1 by Siri Willems, Ishika Jain, Jens De Winne, Siri Luthman, Hamed Zivari Adab, Roeland Vandebriel, Anna Alperovich, Xiaohan Zhang, Ben Stoffelen, Tom De Schepper, Lien Solie, Steven De Vleeschouwer

Current image-guided neuronavigation techniques, such as intraoperative MRI and ultrasound, are limited by high costs, limited sensitivity and operational complexity. There is a clear need for more efficient systems to enhance intra-operative tumor detection and improve patient outcomes for intrinsic brain tumor resection. Whereas previous intraoperative brain HSI studies have predominantly investigated high-grade gliomas, glioblastomas, or mixed-grade cohorts, this clinical study focuses specifically on low-grade gliomas (LGGs). LGGs strongly resemble normal brain under white light conditions on the operative microscope. This study characterizes spectral differences between healthy and tumor tissue and provides a paired comparison of HSI- and RGB-based classification at matched tissue locations and under identical patient-level data splits. Tumor regions showed consistently lower reflectance, which may be associated with differences in hemoglobin concentration and oxygenation. A range of classification models was evaluated. The 3D CNN, which integrates spectral and spatial features, achieved the highest median ROC-AUC of 74.5%, compared with 67.9% for the spectral-only XGBoost model and 64.0% for the RGB-based model. These results support further investigation of HSI for low-grade glioma classification and quantify its performance relative to conventional RGB imaging in this cohort.

##### **Evaluating the Causal Faithfulness of SHAP as a Candidate Attribution Method for SAE Features**
2610.17898v1 by Samuel Xie, Noura Al-Moubayed

Sparse autoencoders decompose language model representations into interpretable features, but feature interpretation in this literature remains predominantly correlational. Attribution methods offer more direct estimates of feature influence, yet their faithfulness to actual causal effects has not been systematically evaluated in the SAE setting. We apply SHAP-based attribution to SAE latent features and evaluate it against direct causal intervention, using sentiment classification on SST-2 with GPT-2 Small and a 32,768-feature layer-7 SAE as a testbed. We compare SHAP to Integrated Gradients, Gradient$\times$Input, and probe-weight baselines across linear and MLP probe arms, scoring each against feature ablation and activation steering measured through the language model's own logit difference. Most faithfulness associations we measure do not survive multiple-comparison correction, and attribution methods that agree strongly with one another ($ρ= 0.42$--$0.80$) agree poorly with measured causal effects, indicating that inter-method agreement is not evidence of causal faithfulness. Two effects hold under control. First, local per-example attribution retains a directional advantage over global averaged attribution, while its apparent importance advantage is explained by feature activity rather than superior causal ranking. Second, under known-ground-truth minimal pairs, attribution recovers inserted sentiment signal well above chance (AUROC $0.792$) but fails to localize deletions (AUROC $0.520$), suggesting that these methods detect the presence of a feature's influence more reliably than its absence. We argue that importance faithfulness and directional faithfulness are separable properties requiring separate evaluation, and that attribution agreement should not substitute for causal validation.

##### **A Tutorial on Quantum Amplitude Embedding with Data Re-Uploading**
2610.17836v1 by Hyojun Ahn, Joongheon Kim

Amplitude embedding lets a variational quantum model read many classical features with few qubits, but the model output is then only a quadratic form in the normalized input. Data re-uploading, which encodes the input repeatedly between trainable blocks, can be used to enrich such a model. Because every additional upload of an amplitude embedding repeats a costly state preparation, the gain that an upload buys has to be known before the gates are spent. This tutorial thus addresses the problem of explaining what data re-uploading adds to an amplitude-embedded model and how large the gain is. We express sequential re-uploading and parallel re-uploading with two copies in one notation and show that each upload raises the reachable polynomial order by two. Under one trainable circuit family and one budget of trainable parameters, simulation results show that re-uploading raises the test coefficient of determination from 0.689 to between 0.883 and 0.975 on targets of order four and from 0.337 to between 0.642 and 0.783 on targets of order six, on all ten seeds. On a quadratic target and on handwritten digits, one upload remains the best choice, and every additional upload lowers the gate error that the classifier tolerates. These results help designers match the number of uploads to the polynomial order of the task at hand.

##### **Understanding Gene Expression-to-Image Learning: Spatial Structure and Information Fidelity**
2610.17699v1 by Xiaoqing Lian, Pei Liu, Jiahui Tan, Quan Zou, Chen Lin, Xiangxiang Zeng

Single-cell RNA sequencing (scRNA-seq) enables cell-level analysis of biological heterogeneity, but learning effective representations from high-dimensional, unordered gene expression profiles remains challenging. Gene Expression-to-Image (GE2I) offers an effective learning strategy by arranging gene values on pixel grids for convolutional neural networks, but its underlying mechanisms remain unclear. We study GE2I on full-transcriptome data while keeping the network and training procedure fixed. Our results reveal a trade-off between spatial smoothness and gene-level fidelity. Structured mappings produce smooth, low-frequency patterns that support faster early optimization, whereas assigning multiple genes to the same pixel obscures expression differences and can reduce predictive performance. This trade-off explains why random mappings can compete with structured layouts when many genes are represented. One-to-one gene-to-pixel assignment improves prediction across the tested visual backbones even when image smoothness decreases. We further find that the benefits of pretraining and augmentation depend on the backbone and mapping. These findings provide practical guidance for pixel allocation and the transfer of vision priors to gene expression data.

##### **Assessing the Performance of AI Models in Misinformation Detection Using Predefined Social Noise Constructs**
2610.17469v1 by Manar Alsaid

Social media platforms generate vast volumes of user-generated content that often obscure meaningful discourse, a phenomenon conceptualized as social noise. While existing misinformation detection methods primarily focus on factual verification, they largely overlook the role of social noise in amplifying misinformation. This study assesses the performance of six text-based models and a graph neural network (GNN) in mitigating the effects of misinformation using predefined social noise constructs. The dataset consists of 500,000 tweets collected from the X platform using the Ukraine hashtag during the Russia-Ukraine conflict from March 2024 to March 2025. Advanced machine learning models achieve strong performance, with accuracies ranging from 79.94% to 97.17%. RoBERTa demonstrates the highest performance, with an accuracy of 97.17%, Cohen's kappa of 0.9660, MCC of 0.9660, and AUC of 0.9977. The GNN achieves an accuracy of 64.52% and an AUC of 0.899, demonstrating performance above random-chance classification by exploiting the relational structure between tweets. All text-based models achieve kappa values greater than 0.75, indicating substantial overall agreement. Explainable AI analysis using LIME reveals that 89.6% of predictive weight is attributed to contextual features rather than isolated keywords. The findings highlight that integrating social noise into AI pipelines enhances misinformation detection and supports more effective mitigation strategies in large-scale social media environments.

##### **The belief-action gap in language model agents**
2610.17444v1 by Ayse Sila Okcu, Weisheng Wang, Tongmeng Xie, Niall Dalton, Angelos Nalmpantis, Raghu Arghal, Mario Giulianelli

Language model (LM) agents can fail for different reasons, requiring different explanations and interventions. We study how state beliefs, action values, and action preferences interact as LM agents reason and commit to decisions in text-rendered grid worlds. We estimate task-relevant beliefs using report-based and representation-based probes, and action values using inverse reinforcement learning. We find that task-relevant state and value information is often represented more reliably than it is reflected in behaviour. Tracking action preferences throughout reasoning further shows that decisions sharpen around a commitment point and reported belief uncertainty decreases near commitment, although individual belief changes do not consistently explain shifts in action preference. Finally, forced actions follow reasoning prefixes from other decisions when transplanted, whereas the tested reasoning-window activation edits provide limited control over the action. Together, these results suggest that some failures arise not from missing task-relevant information, but from how available information is translated into action. Our measurements distinguish information availability from belief-action consistency without by themselves identifying the cause of each failure. Our code is available at: https://anonymous.4open.science/r/belief-action-gap-F745/

##### **Learning to Detect Shifts in Unstructured Data Streams via Monitoring-by-Betting**
2610.17399v1 by Siyu Zhou, Jialin Ouyang, Fan Xia, Adarsh Subbaswamy, Drew Prinster, Suchi Saria, Augusto Garcia-Agundez, Sarah Pollet, Jinoos Yazdany, Patrick Vossler, Jean Feng

Continuous monitoring of generative AI systems requires detecting distribution shifts in the unstructured data they ingest and generate. Existing monitoring methods typically control false alarms by requiring the discrepancy measure used to detect shifts to be *pre-specified*. However, for unstructured data, pre-specification creates a sensitivity--breadth trade-off: a narrowly defined measure is sensitive to the anticipated shift but may miss unexpected changes, whereas a broadly defined measure may detect a wider range of shifts but with reduced sensitivity. Although adaptively hypothesizing the shift direction can overcome this limitation, such data-dependent adaptation generally invalidates conventional false-alarm guarantees. We reconcile adaptivity with statistical validity through testing-by-betting, reframing sequential changepoint detection as a betting game in which the bettor continually updates its hypothesized shift direction, or witness function, as new observations arrive. We introduce *asymptotic e-detectors*, which approximate the pre-change distribution using a calibration set of size $m$, and prove that they asymptotically control the average run length to a false alarm as $m$ increases. Empirically, calibration sets as small as $m=30$ provide effective control. We then develop adaptive monitoring strategies ranging from black-box bettors that learn from embeddings to interpretable bettors based on LLM-proposed concepts. Across three real-world datasets, including data from a clinical AI scribe deployment, adaptive monitors detect shifts faster than fixed monitors at the same false-alarm level. Further, when the true shifts are sparse and interpretable, LLM-concept monitors provide the fastest and most interpretable detection, while also guiding root-cause diagnosis for complex shifts.
